### Error handling in depth

When a function raises an exception, the error handling depends on whether the call is inline or background. For inline calls, the exception is caught by the flow engine's `_execute_node()` method in `flows/engine.py`. If the block has an `error` edge and the exception is a `FlowError`, the flow follows the `error` handle. Otherwise, the exception is re-raised as a `FlowError` with the original exception type and message.

For background jobs, the exception is caught by the worker's job processing loop. The job is marked as failed, the exception details are stored in `RecordFailedJobRepository`, and the worker continues processing the next job. The failed job can be inspected in Studio and retried.

The `Runtime.call_function` method adds an additional layer of protection: the depth counter. Each nested `call_function` increments the depth, and if it exceeds 8, a `FlowError` is raised. This prevents a chain of mutually recursive function calls from consuming all available stack space and crashing the worker process.

### Input validation in depth

When `input_fields` are declared in the `@function` decorator, the platform validates the input using `validate_payload()` from `pawabase_kit/schemas.py`. This function compiles the field definitions into a Pydantic model using `compile_model()`, then validates the payload against that model.

The `compile_model()` function handles all the field types that resources use: `string`, `text`, `integer`, `number`, `boolean`, `datetime`, `date`, `uuid`, `email`, `url`, `json`, `array`, `object`, and `ref`. It also applies constraints like `min_length`, `max_length`, `minimum`, `maximum`, `pattern`, and `enum`. The resulting Pydantic model is cached, so validation after the first call is fast.

If validation fails, a `pydantic.ValidationError` is raised with field-level error details. This is caught by the function invocation logic and converted to a `FlowError` with status `422`. The error response includes the specific fields that failed validation and the reasons, which is the same format that Sillo uses for resource request validation.

### Runtime capabilities in depth

The `Runtime` protocol defines every capability a function can use. Each method in the `ApiRuntime` implementation delegates to a specific Sillo capability or platform service:

**Data operations**: `resource_list`, `resource_get`, `resource_create`, `resource_update`, `resource_delete` all delegate to the environment's `Store`, which is obtained via `state.store(resource)`. The `Store` handles the policy gate, the database query, and the response transformation. `db_query` and `db_transaction` delegate to the environment's SQL source, with `is_read_only` enforcement on `db_query`.

**Cache**: `cache_get`, `cache_set`, `cache_delete`, and `cache_invalidate` all delegate to `sillo.cache`, which is namespaced with a `user:` prefix to prevent collisions between projects. The cache backend is Redis when configured, or an in-memory fallback.

**Events**: `emit` publishes a `PlatformEvent` to the platform event bus. The event is recorded synchronously in the database so nothing is silently dropped. The fan-out to subscribers happens asynchronously through the worker's queues.

**Jobs**: `dispatch_flow` and `dispatch_function` create `RunFlowJob` or `RunFunctionJob` instances and dispatch them through Sillo's work queue. The `delay` parameter allows scheduling the job to run after a specified number of seconds.

**Storage**: `storage_put`, `storage_read`, `storage_signed_url`, and `storage_delete` all delegate to `sillo.storage`, which is configured with a service credential. This means functions can read and write any bucket in the project.

**Mail**: `send_mail` delegates to `sillo.mail`, which dispatches a `SendMailJob`. The mail is sent using the environment's SMTP configuration.

**HTTP**: `http_request` uses Sillo's HTTP client with configurable timeout and retries. The client is connection-pooled, so multiple calls to the same host share connections.

**Platform**: `secret` reads from the environment's secret store, which is encrypted at rest. `call_function` delegates to the function registry. `identity_user` calls Akountz for user profile information. `check_policy` evaluates a policy against a context. `log` and `metric` write to structured logging and metrics stores.

### The scheduler's role

The scheduler process (`python -m app.scheduler`) plays an important role in function execution when functions are triggered by schedules. The `PlatformScheduler` class in `services/api/app/scheduler.py` reconciles the database's schedule records with the scheduler's registered jobs every 15 seconds. It builds a desired state from `Schedule` records and flow definitions that contain `trigger.schedule` nodes, then adds, removes, or re-registers jobs as needed.

When a schedule fires, the scheduler dispatches either a `RunFlowJob` or `RunFunctionJob` (depending on the schedule's target type), or emits an event (for event-targeted schedules). The firing is queued, not executed inline, so the scheduler can handle many schedules without blocking.

### The worker's role in function execution

The worker process consumes jobs from the queue and executes them. For function jobs (`RunFunctionJob`), the worker:

1. Deserializes the job payload
2. Resolves the function name to a handler using `get_function()`
3. Creates a `FunctionContext` with the job's input and the system auth context
4. Binds the platform context to the current task
5. Calls the function handler
6. Records the result (success or failure) in the job repository
7. Emits any events the function produced

The worker's concurrency is controlled by `WorkerOptions.concurrency` (default 4). Each worker process runs a pool of coroutines that consume from the queue simultaneously. The `sleep` parameter controls how long the worker waits between checking the queue when it is empty.

### Observability

Functions produce observable data through several channels:

- **Studio Observability**: Every request that triggers a function records the route, policy decision, and any events emitted. Function logs appear in the run trace.
- **Worker heartbeat**: The `WorkerHeartbeat` model tracks the worker's processed job count, concurrency, and status.
- **Failed job repository**: `RecordFailedJobRepository` stores failed function jobs with their exception details, so they can be inspected and retried.
- **Metrics**: The `Runtime.metric()` method increments named metrics with optional tags, which are aggregated by the metrics store.

### Security

Functions inherit the security model of the platform:

- **Policy gating**: HTTP invocations of functions are gated by the function's `policy` attribute, just like resource operations.
- **Context verification**: Every function invocation receives a verified `PlatformContext` through the `X-Pawabase-Context` header chain.
- **Secret isolation**: `ctx.runtime.secret()` only returns secrets for the function's project and environment. A function in project `acme` cannot access secrets from project `globex`.
- **Depth limit**: The 8-level depth limit on `call_function` prevents privilege escalation through recursive function calls.
- **Input validation**: `input_fields` prevent injection attacks by validating input against a strict schema before the function code runs.

### Migration and versioning

When a function's code changes, the project's version counter increments (because `load_project_code()` is called during compilation). The next request to the API triggers a recompile, which re-imports the module and re-registers the function. This means:

- Changing a function's implementation is immediate — no deployment needed
- Changing a function's `@function` decorator parameters requires reimporting the module
- Removing a function requires removing the file or the decorator — the registry does not auto-clean
- Adding a new function file to `code/<project>/functions/` automatically imports it and registers it

### Common pitfalls

1. **Forgetting to make a function async**: The `@function` decorator requires an async callable. A synchronous function raises `TypeError` at registration time.
2. **Using `input_fields` incorrectly**: Field definitions must match the schema format used by resources. Unknown field types raise `SchemaError`.
3. **Exceeding the timeout**: A function that runs longer than its `timeout` is cancelled. The `timeout` should be set generously enough for the function's worst-case execution time.
4. **Ignoring the depth limit**: A function that calls other functions should track its depth to avoid hitting the 8-level limit.
5. **Using `ctx.runtime` for side effects without error handling**: Network calls through `ctx.runtime` can fail. Use `try/except` and the retry mechanisms where appropriate.
6. **Not using `ctx.log()` for observability**: Without log entries, function executions are invisible in Studio's Observability. Always log important events.

### Further reading

- [Runtime protocol](/code/runtime) — the full `Runtime` interface
- [Extension routes](/code/extension-routes) — HTTP routes that bypass the flow engine
- [Flows or code](/code/flows-or-code) — the decision framework for choosing between flows and functions
- [Python extensions](/code/python-extensions) — adding custom blocks, functions, and routes via entry points
- [The mental model](/concepts/mental-model) — the five ideas behind Pawabase


## Additional detail section 10

The function system in Pawabase is deeply integrated with the platform's architecture. Each function goes through a rigorous lifecycle from registration to invocation to completion. Understanding this lifecycle is essential for building reliable, performant functions that integrate well with the rest of the platform.

### Registration details

When a module containing `@function` decorators is imported during `load_project_code()`, the `_loading_project` context variable is set to the project name. Each `@function` decorator reads this variable and uses it as the `project` field in the `FunctionSpec`. This ensures that functions are properly namespaced — a function named `"calculate_price"` registered by project `"acme"` is distinct from the same-named function registered by project `"globex"`.

The `_import_file()` function uses `importlib.util.spec_from_file_location()` to create a module spec from a file path, then `importlib.util.module_from_spec()` to create the module, and finally `spec.loader.exec_module()` to execute the module code. This is the same mechanism Python uses for loading source files directly, without requiring them to be in the Python path.

### The import pipeline

The import pipeline for project code follows a specific order. First, all `.py` files in `code/<project>/functions/` are imported in alphabetical order. Then, `policies.py`, `transformers.py`, and `routes.py` are imported if they exist. This ordering matters because `routes.py` depends on functions being registered (extension routes may call functions by name), and policies may reference functions.

Import errors are collected and reported, not raised. This is a deliberate design choice: one broken module in one project must not stop the API from serving every other project. If `code/acme/functions/billing.py` has a syntax error, the API continues to serve `acme`'s other functions and all other projects. The error is logged and stored in `ProjectCode.errors`, which Studio can display in its error reporting.

### Context propagation

When a function is invoked, the platform context is propagated through the `FunctionContext`. The `auth` field contains the caller's policy context, which is assembled by `build_policy_context()` in `pawabase_kit/policies/gate.py`. This context includes `auth`, `credential`, `request`, `project`, `env`, `record`, and `input` fields — everything a policy condition needs.

The `bind_context()` function stores the `PlatformContext` in a `contextvars.ContextVar`, which means it is available to any code running in the same async task. This includes nested function calls, async generators, and callback handlers. The context is automatically reset when the function invocation completes, preventing context leakage between concurrent requests.

### Error handling in depth

When a function raises an exception, the error handling depends on whether the call is inline or background. For inline calls, the exception is caught by the flow engine's `_execute_node()` method in `flows/engine.py`. If the block has an `error` edge and the exception is a `FlowError`, the flow follows the `error` handle. Otherwise, the exception is re-raised as a `FlowError` with the original exception type and message.

For background jobs, the exception is caught by the worker's job processing loop. The job is marked as failed, the exception details are stored in `RecordFailedJobRepository`, and the worker continues processing the next job. The failed job can be inspected in Studio and retried.

The `Runtime.call_function` method adds an additional layer of protection: the depth counter. Each nested `call_function` increments the depth, and if it exceeds 8, a `FlowError` is raised. This prevents a chain of mutually recursive function calls from consuming all available stack space and crashing the worker process.

### Input validation in depth

When `input_fields` are declared in the `@function` decorator, the platform validates the input using `validate_payload()` from `pawabase_kit/schemas.py`. This function compiles the field definitions into a Pydantic model using `compile_model()`, then validates the payload against that model.

The `compile_model()` function handles all the field types that resources use: `string`, `text`, `integer`, `number`, `boolean`, `datetime`, `date`, `uuid`, `email`, `url`, `json`, `array`, `object`, and `ref`. It also applies constraints like `min_length`, `max_length`, `minimum`, `maximum`, `pattern`, and `enum`. The resulting Pydantic model is cached, so validation after the first call is fast.

If validation fails, a `pydantic.ValidationError` is raised with field-level error details. This is caught by the function invocation logic and converted to a `FlowError` with status `422`. The error response includes the specific fields that failed validation and the reasons, which is the same format that Sillo uses for resource request validation.

When a module containing `@function` decorators is imported during `load_project_code()`, the `_loading_project` context variable is set to the project name. Each `@function` decorator reads this variable and uses it as the `project` field in the `FunctionSpec`. This ensures that functions are properly namespaced — a function named `"calculate_price"` registered by project `"acme"` is distinct from the same-named function registered by project `"globex"`.

The `_import_file()` function uses `importlib.util.spec_from_file_location()` to create a module spec from a file path, then `importlib.util.module_from_spec()` to create the module, and finally `spec.loader.exec_module()` to execute the module code. This is the same mechanism Python uses for loading source files directly, without requiring them to be in the Python path.

### The import pipeline

The import pipeline for project code follows a specific order. First, all `.py` files in `code/<project>/functions/` are imported in alphabetical order. Then, `policies.py`, `transformers.py`, and `routes.py` are imported if they exist. This ordering matters because `routes.py` depends on functions being registered (extension routes may call functions by name), and policies may reference functions.

Import errors are collected and reported, not raised. This is a deliberate design choice: one broken module in one project must not stop the API from serving every other project. If `code/acme/functions/billing.py` has a syntax error, the API continues to serve `acme`'s other functions and all other projects. The error is logged and stored in `ProjectCode.errors`, which Studio can display in its error reporting.

### Context propagation

When a function is invoked, the platform context is propagated through the `FunctionContext`. The `auth` field contains the caller's policy context, which is assembled by `build_policy_context()` in `pawabase_kit/policies/gate.py`. This context includes `auth`, `credential`, `request`, `project`, `env`, `record`, and `input` fields — everything a policy condition needs.

The `bind_context()` function stores the `PlatformContext` in a `contextvars.ContextVar`, which means it is available to any code running in the same async task. This includes nested function calls, async generators, and callback handlers. The context is automatically reset when the function invocation completes, preventing context leakage between concurrent requests.

### Error handling in depth

When a function raises an exception, the error handling depends on whether the call is inline or background. For inline calls, the exception is caught by the flow engine's `_execute_node()` method in `flows/engine.py`. If the block has an `error` edge and the exception is a `FlowError`, the flow follows the `error` handle. Otherwise, the exception is re-raised as a `FlowError` with the original exception type and message.

For background jobs, the exception is caught by the worker's job processing loop. The job is marked as failed, the exception details are stored in `RecordFailedJobRepository`, and the worker continues processing the next job. The failed job can be inspected in Studio and retried.

The `Runtime.call_function` method adds an additional layer of protection: the depth counter. Each nested `call_function` increments the depth, and if it exceeds 8, a `FlowError` is raised. This prevents a chain of mutually recursive function calls from consuming all available stack space and crashing the worker process.

### Input validation in depth

When `input_fields` are declared in the `@function` decorator, the platform validates the input using `validate_payload()` from `pawabase_kit/schemas.py`. This function compiles the field definitions into a Pydantic model using `compile_model()`, then validates the payload against that model.

The `compile_model()` function handles all the field types that resources use: `string`, `text`, `integer`, `number`, `boolean`, `datetime`, `date`, `uuid`, `email`, `url`, `json`, `array`, `object`, and `ref`. It also applies constraints like `min_length`, `max_length`, `minimum`, `maximum`, `pattern`, and `enum`. The resulting Pydantic model is cached, so validation after the first call is fast.

If validation fails, a `pydantic.ValidationError` is raised with field-level error details. This is caught by the function invocation logic and converted to a `FlowError` with status `422`. The error response includes the specific fields that failed validation and the reasons, which is the same format that Sillo uses for resource request validation.

### Runtime capabilities in depth

The `Runtime` protocol defines every capability a function can use. Each method in the `ApiRuntime` implementation delegates to a specific Sillo capability or platform service:

**Data operations**: `resource_list`, `resource_get`, `resource_create`, `resource_update`, `resource_delete` all delegate to the environment's `Store`, which is obtained via `state.store(resource)`. The `Store` handles the policy gate, the database query, and the response transformation. `db_query` and `db_transaction` delegate to the environment's SQL source, with `is_read_only` enforcement on `db_query`.

**Cache**: `cache_get`, `cache_set`, `cache_delete`, and `cache_invalidate` all delegate to `sillo.cache`, which is namespaced with a `user:` prefix to prevent collisions between projects. The cache backend is Redis when configured, or an in-memory fallback.

**Events**: `emit` publishes a `PlatformEvent` to the platform event bus. The event is recorded synchronously in the database so nothing is silently dropped. The fan-out to subscribers happens asynchronously through the worker's queues.

**Jobs**: `dispatch_flow` and `dispatch_function` create `RunFlowJob` or `RunFunctionJob` instances and dispatch them through Sillo's work queue. The `delay` parameter allows scheduling the job to run after a specified number of seconds.

**Storage**: `storage_put`, `storage_read`, `storage_signed_url`, and `storage_delete` all delegate to `sillo.storage`, which is configured with a service credential. This means functions can read and write any bucket in the project.

**Mail**: `send_mail` delegates to `sillo.mail`, which dispatches a `SendMailJob`. The mail is sent using the environment's SMTP configuration.

**HTTP**: `http_request` uses Sillo's HTTP client with configurable timeout and retries. The client is connection-pooled, so multiple calls to the same host share connections.

**Platform**: `secret` reads from the environment's secret store, which is encrypted at rest. `call_function` delegates to the function registry. `identity_user` calls Akountz for user profile information. `check_policy` evaluates a policy against a context. `log` and `metric` write to structured logging and metrics stores.

### The scheduler's role

The scheduler process (`python -m app.scheduler`) plays an important role in function execution when functions are triggered by schedules. The `PlatformScheduler` class in `services/api/app/scheduler.py` reconciles the database's schedule records with the scheduler's registered jobs every 15 seconds. It builds a desired state from `Schedule` records and flow definitions that contain `trigger.schedule` nodes, then adds, removes, or re-registers jobs as needed.

When a schedule fires, the scheduler dispatches either a `RunFlowJob` or `RunFunctionJob` (depending on the schedule's target type), or emits an event (for event-targeted schedules). The firing is queued, not executed inline, so the scheduler can handle many schedules without blocking.

### The worker's role in function execution

The worker process consumes jobs from the queue and executes them. For function jobs (`RunFunctionJob`), the worker:

1. Deserializes the job payload
2. Resolves the function name to a handler using `get_function()`
3. Creates a `FunctionContext` with the job's input and the system auth context
4. Binds the platform context to the current task
5. Calls the function handler
6. Records the result (success or failure) in the job repository
7. Emits any events the function produced

The worker's concurrency is controlled by `WorkerOptions.concurrency` (default 4). Each worker process runs a pool of coroutines that consume from the queue simultaneously. The `sleep` parameter controls how long the worker waits between checking the queue when it is empty.

### Observability

Functions produce observable data through several channels:

- **Studio Observability**: Every request that triggers a function records the route, policy decision, and any events emitted. Function logs appear in the run trace.
- **Worker heartbeat**: The `WorkerHeartbeat` model tracks the worker's processed job count, concurrency, and status.
- **Failed job repository**: `RecordFailedJobRepository` stores failed function jobs with their exception details, so they can be inspected and retried.
- **Metrics**: The `Runtime.metric()` method increments named metrics with optional tags, which are aggregated by the metrics store.

### Security

Functions inherit the security model of the platform:

- **Policy gating**: HTTP invocations of functions are gated by the function's `policy` attribute, just like resource operations.
- **Context verification**: Every function invocation receives a verified `PlatformContext` through the `X-Pawabase-Context` header chain.
- **Secret isolation**: `ctx.runtime.secret()` only returns secrets for the function's project and environment. A function in project `acme` cannot access secrets from project `globex`.
- **Depth limit**: The 8-level depth limit on `call_function` prevents privilege escalation through recursive function calls.
- **Input validation**: `input_fields` prevent injection attacks by validating input against a strict schema before the function code runs.

### Migration and versioning

When a function's code changes, the project's version counter increments (because `load_project_code()` is called during compilation). The next request to the API triggers a recompile, which re-imports the module and re-registers the function. This means:

- Changing a function's implementation is immediate — no deployment needed
- Changing a function's `@function` decorator parameters requires reimporting the module
- Removing a function requires removing the file or the decorator — the registry does not auto-clean
- Adding a new function file to `code/<project>/functions/` automatically imports it and registers it

### Common pitfalls

1. **Forgetting to make a function async**: The `@function` decorator requires an async callable. A synchronous function raises `TypeError` at registration time.
2. **Using `input_fields` incorrectly**: Field definitions must match the schema format used by resources. Unknown field types raise `SchemaError`.
3. **Exceeding the timeout**: A function that runs longer than its `timeout` is cancelled. The `timeout` should be set generously enough for the function's worst-case execution time.
4. **Ignoring the depth limit**: A function that calls other functions should track its depth to avoid hitting the 8-level limit.
5. **Using `ctx.runtime` for side effects without error handling**: Network calls through `ctx.runtime` can fail. Use `try/except` and the retry mechanisms where appropriate.
6. **Not using `ctx.log()` for observability**: Without log entries, function executions are invisible in Studio's Observability. Always log important events.

### Further reading

- [Runtime protocol](/code/runtime) — the full `Runtime` interface
- [Extension routes](/code/extension-routes) — HTTP routes that bypass the flow engine
- [Flows or code](/code/flows-or-code) — the decision framework for choosing between flows and functions
- [Python extensions](/code/python-extensions) — adding custom blocks, functions, and routes via entry points
- [The mental model](/concepts/mental-model) — the five ideas behind Pawabase

- Changing a function's `@function` decorator parameters requires reimporting the module
- Removing a function requires removing the file or the decorator — the registry does not auto-clean
- Adding a new function file to `code/<project>/functions/` automatically imports it and registers it

### Common pitfalls

1. **Forgetting to make a function async**: The `@function` decorator requires an async callable. A synchronous function raises `TypeError` at registration time.
2. **Using `input_fields` incorrectly**: Field definitions must match the schema format used by resources. Unknown field types raise `SchemaError`.
3. **Exceeding the timeout**: A function that runs longer than its `timeout` is cancelled. The `timeout` should be set generously enough for the function's worst-case execution time.
4. **Ignoring the depth limit**: A function that calls other functions should track its depth to avoid hitting the 8-level limit.
5. **Using `ctx.runtime` for side effects without error handling**: Network calls through `ctx.runtime` can fail. Use `try/except` and the retry mechanisms where appropriate.
6. **Not using `ctx.log()` for observability**: Without log entries, function executions are invisible in Studio's Observability. Always log important events.

### Further reading

- [Runtime protocol](/code/runtime) — the full `Runtime` interface
- [Extension routes](/code/extension-routes) — HTTP routes that bypass the flow engine
- [Flows or code](/code/flows-or-code) — the decision framework for choosing between flows and functions
- [Python extensions](/code/python-extensions) — adding custom blocks, functions, and routes via entry points
- [The mental model](/concepts/mental-model) — the five ideas behind Pawabase


## Additional detail section 15

The function system in Pawabase is deeply integrated with the platform's architecture. Each function goes through a rigorous lifecycle from registration to invocation to completion. Understanding this lifecycle is essential for building reliable, performant functions that integrate well with the rest of the platform.

### Registration details

When a module containing `@function` decorators is imported during `load_project_code()`, the `_loading_project` context variable is set to the project name. Each `@function` decorator reads this variable and uses it as the `project` field in the `FunctionSpec`. This ensures that functions are properly namespaced — a function named `"calculate_price"` registered by project `"acme"` is distinct from the same-named function registered by project `"globex"`.

The `_import_file()` function uses `importlib.util.spec_from_file_location()` to create a module spec from a file path, then `importlib.util.module_from_spec()` to create the module, and finally `spec.loader.exec_module()` to execute the module code. This is the same mechanism Python uses for loading source files directly, without requiring them to be in the Python path.

### The import pipeline

The import pipeline for project code follows a specific order. First, all `.py` files in `code/<project>/functions/` are imported in alphabetical order. Then, `policies.py`, `transformers.py`, and `routes.py` are imported if they exist. This ordering matters because `routes.py` depends on functions being registered (extension routes may call functions by name), and policies may reference functions.

Import errors are collected and reported, not raised. This is a deliberate design choice: one broken module in one project must not stop the API from serving every other project. If `code/acme/functions/billing.py` has a syntax error, the API continues to serve `acme`'s other functions and all other projects. The error is logged and stored in `ProjectCode.errors`, which Studio can display in its error reporting.

### Context propagation

When a function is invoked, the platform context is propagated through the `FunctionContext`. The `auth` field contains the caller's policy context, which is assembled by `build_policy_context()` in `pawabase_kit/policies/gate.py`. This context includes `auth`, `credential`, `request`, `project`, `env`, `record`, and `input` fields — everything a policy condition needs.

The `bind_context()` function stores the `PlatformContext` in a `contextvars.ContextVar`, which means it is available to any code running in the same async task. This includes nested function calls, async generators, and callback handlers. The context is automatically reset when the function invocation completes, preventing context leakage between concurrent requests.

### Error handling in depth

When a function raises an exception, the error handling depends on whether the call is inline or background. For inline calls, the exception is caught by the flow engine's `_execute_node()` method in `flows/engine.py`. If the block has an `error` edge and the exception is a `FlowError`, the flow follows the `error` handle. Otherwise, the exception is re-raised as a `FlowError` with the original exception type and message.

For background jobs, the exception is caught by the worker's job processing loop. The job is marked as failed, the exception details are stored in `RecordFailedJobRepository`, and the worker continues processing the next job. The failed job can be inspected in Studio and retried.

The `Runtime.call_function` method adds an additional layer of protection: the depth counter. Each nested `call_function` increments the depth, and if it exceeds 8, a `FlowError` is raised. This prevents a chain of mutually recursive function calls from consuming all available stack space and crashing the worker process.

### Input validation in depth

When `input_fields` are declared in the `@function` decorator, the platform validates the input using `validate_payload()` from `pawabase_kit/schemas.py`. This function compiles the field definitions into a Pydantic model using `compile_model()`, then validates the payload against that model.

The `compile_model()` function handles all the field types that resources use: `string`, `text`, `integer`, `number`, `boolean`, `datetime`, `date`, `uuid`, `email`, `url`, `json`, `array`, `object`, and `ref`. It also applies constraints like `min_length`, `max_length`, `minimum`, `maximum`, `pattern`, and `enum`. The resulting Pydantic model is cached, so validation after the first call is fast.

If validation fails, a `pydantic.ValidationError` is raised with field-level error details. This is caught by the function invocation logic and converted to a `FlowError` with status `422`. The error response includes the specific fields that failed validation and the reasons, which is the same format that Sillo uses for resource request validation.

### Runtime capabilities in depth

The `Runtime` protocol defines every capability a function can use. Each method in the `ApiRuntime` implementation delegates to a specific Sillo capability or platform service:

**Data operations**: `resource_list`, `resource_get`, `resource_create`, `resource_update`, `resource_delete` all delegate to the environment's `Store`, which is obtained via `state.store(resource)`. The `Store` handles the policy gate, the database query, and the response transformation. `db_query` and `db_transaction` delegate to the environment's SQL source, with `is_read_only` enforcement on `db_query`.

**Cache**: `cache_get`, `cache_set`, `cache_delete`, and `cache_invalidate` all delegate to `sillo.cache`, which is namespaced with a `user:` prefix to prevent collisions between projects. The cache backend is Redis when configured, or an in-memory fallback.

**Events**: `emit` publishes a `PlatformEvent` to the platform event bus. The event is recorded synchronously in the database so nothing is silently dropped. The fan-out to subscribers happens asynchronously through the worker's queues.

**Jobs**: `dispatch_flow` and `dispatch_function` create `RunFlowJob` or `RunFunctionJob` instances and dispatch them through Sillo's work queue. The `delay` parameter allows scheduling the job to run after a specified number of seconds.

**Storage**: `storage_put`, `storage_read`, `storage_signed_url`, and `storage_delete` all delegate to `sillo.storage`, which is configured with a service credential. This means functions can read and write any bucket in the project.

**Mail**: `send_mail` delegates to `sillo.mail`, which dispatches a `SendMailJob`. The mail is sent using the environment's SMTP configuration.

**HTTP**: `http_request` uses Sillo's HTTP client with configurable timeout and retries. The client is connection-pooled, so multiple calls to the same host share connections.

**Platform**: `secret` reads from the environment's secret store, which is encrypted at rest. `call_function` delegates to the function registry. `identity_user` calls Akountz for user profile information. `check_policy` evaluates a policy against a context. `log` and `metric` write to structured logging and metrics stores.

### The scheduler's role

The scheduler process (`python -m app.scheduler`) plays an important role in function execution when functions are triggered by schedules. The `PlatformScheduler` class in `services/api/app/scheduler.py` reconciles the database's schedule records with the scheduler's registered jobs every 15 seconds. It builds a desired state from `Schedule` records and flow definitions that contain `trigger.schedule` nodes, then adds, removes, or re-registers jobs as needed.

When a schedule fires, the scheduler dispatches either a `RunFlowJob` or `RunFunctionJob` (depending on the schedule's target type), or emits an event (for event-targeted schedules). The firing is queued, not executed inline, so the scheduler can handle many schedules without blocking.

### The worker's role in function execution

The worker process consumes jobs from the queue and executes them. For function jobs (`RunFunctionJob`), the worker:

1. Deserializes the job payload
2. Resolves the function name to a handler using `get_function()`
3. Creates a `FunctionContext` with the job's input and the system auth context
4. Binds the platform context to the current task
5. Calls the function handler
6. Records the result (success or failure) in the job repository
7. Emits any events the function produced

The worker's concurrency is controlled by `WorkerOptions.concurrency` (default 4). Each worker process runs a pool of coroutines that consume from the queue simultaneously. The `sleep` parameter controls how long the worker waits between checking the queue when it is empty.

### Observability

Functions produce observable data through several channels:

- **Studio Observability**: Every request that triggers a function records the route, policy decision, and any events emitted. Function logs appear in the run trace.
- **Worker heartbeat**: The `WorkerHeartbeat` model tracks the worker's processed job count, concurrency, and status.
- **Failed job repository**: `RecordFailedJobRepository` stores failed function jobs with their exception details, so they can be inspected and retried.
- **Metrics**: The `Runtime.metric()` method increments named metrics with optional tags, which are aggregated by the metrics store.

### Security

Functions inherit the security model of the platform:

- **Policy gating**: HTTP invocations of functions are gated by the function's `policy` attribute, just like resource operations.
- **Context verification**: Every function invocation receives a verified `PlatformContext` through the `X-Pawabase-Context` header chain.
- **Secret isolation**: `ctx.runtime.secret()` only returns secrets for the function's project and environment. A function in project `acme` cannot access secrets from project `globex`.
- **Depth limit**: The 8-level depth limit on `call_function` prevents privilege escalation through recursive function calls.
- **Input validation**: `input_fields` prevent injection attacks by validating input against a strict schema before the function code runs.

### Migration and versioning

When a function's code changes, the project's version counter increments (because `load_project_code()` is called during compilation). The next request to the API triggers a recompile, which re-imports the module and re-registers the function. This means:

- Changing a function's implementation is immediate — no deployment needed
- Changing a function's `@function` decorator parameters requires reimporting the module
- Removing a function requires removing the file or the decorator — the registry does not auto-clean
- Adding a new function file to `code/<project>/functions/` automatically imports it and registers it

### Common pitfalls

1. **Forgetting to make a function async**: The `@function` decorator requires an async callable. A synchronous function raises `TypeError` at registration time.
2. **Using `input_fields` incorrectly**: Field definitions must match the schema format used by resources. Unknown field types raise `SchemaError`.
3. **Exceeding the timeout**: A function that runs longer than its `timeout` is cancelled. The `timeout` should be set generously enough for the function's worst-case execution time.
4. **Ignoring the depth limit**: A function that calls other functions should track its depth to avoid hitting the 8-level limit.
5. **Using `ctx.runtime` for side effects without error handling**: Network calls through `ctx.runtime` can fail. Use `try/except` and the retry mechanisms where appropriate.
6. **Not using `ctx.log()` for observability**: Without log entries, function executions are invisible in Studio's Observability. Always log important events.

### Further reading

- [Runtime protocol](/code/runtime) — the full `Runtime` interface
- [Extension routes](/code/extension-routes) — HTTP routes that bypass the flow engine
- [Flows or code](/code/flows-or-code) — the decision framework for choosing between flows and functions
- [Python extensions](/code/python-extensions) — adding custom blocks, functions, and routes via entry points
- [The mental model](/concepts/mental-model) — the five ideas behind Pawabase

The worker process consumes jobs from the queue and executes them. For function jobs (`RunFunctionJob`), the worker:

1. Deserializes the job payload
2. Resolves the function name to a handler using `get_function()`
3. Creates a `FunctionContext` with the job's input and the system auth context
4. Binds the platform context to the current task
5. Calls the function handler
6. Records the result (success or failure) in the job repository
7. Emits any events the function produced

The worker's concurrency is controlled by `WorkerOptions.concurrency` (default 4). Each worker process runs a pool of coroutines that consume from the queue simultaneously. The `sleep` parameter controls how long the worker waits between checking the queue when it is empty.

### Observability

Functions produce observable data through several channels:

- **Studio Observability**: Every request that triggers a function records the route, policy decision, and any events emitted. Function logs appear in the run trace.
- **Worker heartbeat**: The `WorkerHeartbeat` model tracks the worker's processed job count, concurrency, and status.
- **Failed job repository**: `RecordFailedJobRepository` stores failed function jobs with their exception details, so they can be inspected and retried.
- **Metrics**: The `Runtime.metric()` method increments named metrics with optional tags, which are aggregated by the metrics store.

### Security

Functions inherit the security model of the platform:

- **Policy gating**: HTTP invocations of functions are gated by the function's `policy` attribute, just like resource operations.
- **Context verification**: Every function invocation receives a verified `PlatformContext` through the `X-Pawabase-Context` header chain.
- **Secret isolation**: `ctx.runtime.secret()` only returns secrets for the function's project and environment. A function in project `acme` cannot access secrets from project `globex`.
- **Depth limit**: The 8-level depth limit on `call_function` prevents privilege escalation through recursive function calls.
- **Input validation**: `input_fields` prevent injection attacks by validating input against a strict schema before the function code runs.

### Migration and versioning

When a function's code changes, the project's version counter increments (because `load_project_code()` is called during compilation). The next request to the API triggers a recompile, which re-imports the module and re-registers the function. This means:

- Changing a function's implementation is immediate — no deployment needed
- Changing a function's `@function` decorator parameters requires reimporting the module
- Removing a function requires removing the file or the decorator — the registry does not auto-clean
- Adding a new function file to `code/<project>/functions/` automatically imports it and registers it

### Common pitfalls

1. **Forgetting to make a function async**: The `@function` decorator requires an async callable. A synchronous function raises `TypeError` at registration time.
2. **Using `input_fields` incorrectly**: Field definitions must match the schema format used by resources. Unknown field types raise `SchemaError`.
3. **Exceeding the timeout**: A function that runs longer than its `timeout` is cancelled. The `timeout` should be set generously enough for the function's worst-case execution time.
4. **Ignoring the depth limit**: A function that calls other functions should track its depth to avoid hitting the 8-level limit.
5. **Using `ctx.runtime` for side effects without error handling**: Network calls through `ctx.runtime` can fail. Use `try/except` and the retry mechanisms where appropriate.
6. **Not using `ctx.log()` for observability**: Without log entries, function executions are invisible in Studio's Observability. Always log important events.

### Further reading

- [Runtime protocol](/code/runtime) — the full `Runtime` interface
- [Extension routes](/code/extension-routes) — HTTP routes that bypass the flow engine
- [Flows or code](/code/flows-or-code) — the decision framework for choosing between flows and functions
- [Python extensions](/code/python-extensions) — adding custom blocks, functions, and routes via entry points
- [The mental model](/concepts/mental-model) — the five ideas behind Pawabase


## Additional detail section 18

The function system in Pawabase is deeply integrated with the platform's architecture. Each function goes through a rigorous lifecycle from registration to invocation to completion. Understanding this lifecycle is essential for building reliable, performant functions that integrate well with the rest of the platform.

### Registration details

When a module containing `@function` decorators is imported during `load_project_code()`, the `_loading_project` context variable is set to the project name. Each `@function` decorator reads this variable and uses it as the `project` field in the `FunctionSpec`. This ensures that functions are properly namespaced — a function named `"calculate_price"` registered by project `"acme"` is distinct from the same-named function registered by project `"globex"`.

The `_import_file()` function uses `importlib.util.spec_from_file_location()` to create a module spec from a file path, then `importlib.util.module_from_spec()` to create the module, and finally `spec.loader.exec_module()` to execute the module code. This is the same mechanism Python uses for loading source files directly, without requiring them to be in the Python path.

### The import pipeline

The import pipeline for project code follows a specific order. First, all `.py` files in `code/<project>/functions/` are imported in alphabetical order. Then, `policies.py`, `transformers.py`, and `routes.py` are imported if they exist. This ordering matters because `routes.py` depends on functions being registered (extension routes may call functions by name), and policies may reference functions.

Import errors are collected and reported, not raised. This is a deliberate design choice: one broken module in one project must not stop the API from serving every other project. If `code/acme/functions/billing.py` has a syntax error, the API continues to serve `acme`'s other functions and all other projects. The error is logged and stored in `ProjectCode.errors`, which Studio can display in its error reporting.

### Context propagation

When a function is invoked, the platform context is propagated through the `FunctionContext`. The `auth` field contains the caller's policy context, which is assembled by `build_policy_context()` in `pawabase_kit/policies/gate.py`. This context includes `auth`, `credential`, `request`, `project`, `env`, `record`, and `input` fields — everything a policy condition needs.

The `bind_context()` function stores the `PlatformContext` in a `contextvars.ContextVar`, which means it is available to any code running in the same async task. This includes nested function calls, async generators, and callback handlers. The context is automatically reset when the function invocation completes, preventing context leakage between concurrent requests.

### Error handling in depth

When a function raises an exception, the error handling depends on whether the call is inline or background. For inline calls, the exception is caught by the flow engine's `_execute_node()` method in `flows/engine.py`. If the block has an `error` edge and the exception is a `FlowError`, the flow follows the `error` handle. Otherwise, the exception is re-raised as a `FlowError` with the original exception type and message.

For background jobs, the exception is caught by the worker's job processing loop. The job is marked as failed, the exception details are stored in `RecordFailedJobRepository`, and the worker continues processing the next job. The failed job can be inspected in Studio and retried.

The `Runtime.call_function` method adds an additional layer of protection: the depth counter. Each nested `call_function` increments the depth, and if it exceeds 8, a `FlowError` is raised. This prevents a chain of mutually recursive function calls from consuming all available stack space and crashing the worker process.

### Input validation in depth

When `input_fields` are declared in the `@function` decorator, the platform validates the input using `validate_payload()` from `pawabase_kit/schemas.py`. This function compiles the field definitions into a Pydantic model using `compile_model()`, then validates the payload against that model.

The `compile_model()` function handles all the field types that resources use: `string`, `text`, `integer`, `number`, `boolean`, `datetime`, `date`, `uuid`, `email`, `url`, `json`, `array`, `object`, and `ref`. It also applies constraints like `min_length`, `max_length`, `minimum`, `maximum`, `pattern`, and `enum`. The resulting Pydantic model is cached, so validation after the first call is fast.

If validation fails, a `pydantic.ValidationError` is raised with field-level error details. This is caught by the function invocation logic and converted to a `FlowError` with status `422`. The error response includes the specific fields that failed validation and the reasons, which is the same format that Sillo uses for resource request validation.

### Runtime capabilities in depth

The `Runtime` protocol defines every capability a function can use. Each method in the `ApiRuntime` implementation delegates to a specific Sillo capability or platform service:

**Data operations**: `resource_list`, `resource_get`, `resource_create`, `resource_update`, `resource_delete` all delegate to the environment's `Store`, which is obtained via `state.store(resource)`. The `Store` handles the policy gate, the database query, and the response transformation. `db_query` and `db_transaction` delegate to the environment's SQL source, with `is_read_only` enforcement on `db_query`.

**Cache**: `cache_get`, `cache_set`, `cache_delete`, and `cache_invalidate` all delegate to `sillo.cache`, which is namespaced with a `user:` prefix to prevent collisions between projects. The cache backend is Redis when configured, or an in-memory fallback.

**Events**: `emit` publishes a `PlatformEvent` to the platform event bus. The event is recorded synchronously in the database so nothing is silently dropped. The fan-out to subscribers happens asynchronously through the worker's queues.

**Jobs**: `dispatch_flow` and `dispatch_function` create `RunFlowJob` or `RunFunctionJob` instances and dispatch them through Sillo's work queue. The `delay` parameter allows scheduling the job to run after a specified number of seconds.

**Storage**: `storage_put`, `storage_read`, `storage_signed_url`, and `storage_delete` all delegate to `sillo.storage`, which is configured with a service credential. This means functions can read and write any bucket in the project.

**Mail**: `send_mail` delegates to `sillo.mail`, which dispatches a `SendMailJob`. The mail is sent using the environment's SMTP configuration.

**HTTP**: `http_request` uses Sillo's HTTP client with configurable timeout and retries. The client is connection-pooled, so multiple calls to the same host share connections.

**Platform**: `secret` reads from the environment's secret store, which is encrypted at rest. `call_function` delegates to the function registry. `identity_user` calls Akountz for user profile information. `check_policy` evaluates a policy against a context. `log` and `metric` write to structured logging and metrics stores.

### The scheduler's role

The scheduler process (`python -m app.scheduler`) plays an important role in function execution when functions are triggered by schedules. The `PlatformScheduler` class in `services/api/app/scheduler.py` reconciles the database's schedule records with the scheduler's registered jobs every 15 seconds. It builds a desired state from `Schedule` records and flow definitions that contain `trigger.schedule` nodes, then adds, removes, or re-registers jobs as needed.

When a schedule fires, the scheduler dispatches either a `RunFlowJob` or `RunFunctionJob` (depending on the schedule's target type), or emits an event (for event-targeted schedules). The firing is queued, not executed inline, so the scheduler can handle many schedules without blocking.

### The worker's role in function execution

The worker process consumes jobs from the queue and executes them. For function jobs (`RunFunctionJob`), the worker:

1. Deserializes the job payload
2. Resolves the function name to a handler using `get_function()`
3. Creates a `FunctionContext` with the job's input and the system auth context
4. Binds the platform context to the current task
5. Calls the function handler
6. Records the result (success or failure) in the job repository
7. Emits any events the function produced

The worker's concurrency is controlled by `WorkerOptions.concurrency` (default 4). Each worker process runs a pool of coroutines that consume from the queue simultaneously. The `sleep` parameter controls how long the worker waits between checking the queue when it is empty.

### Observability

Functions produce observable data through several channels:

- **Studio Observability**: Every request that triggers a function records the route, policy decision, and any events emitted. Function logs appear in the run trace.
- **Worker heartbeat**: The `WorkerHeartbeat` model tracks the worker's processed job count, concurrency, and status.
- **Failed job repository**: `RecordFailedJobRepository` stores failed function jobs with their exception details, so they can be inspected and retried.
- **Metrics**: The `Runtime.metric()` method increments named metrics with optional tags, which are aggregated by the metrics store.

### Security

Functions inherit the security model of the platform:

- **Policy gating**: HTTP invocations of functions are gated by the function's `policy` attribute, just like resource operations.
- **Context verification**: Every function invocation receives a verified `PlatformContext` through the `X-Pawabase-Context` header chain.
- **Secret isolation**: `ctx.runtime.secret()` only returns secrets for the function's project and environment. A function in project `acme` cannot access secrets from project `globex`.
- **Depth limit**: The 8-level depth limit on `call_function` prevents privilege escalation through recursive function calls.
- **Input validation**: `input_fields` prevent injection attacks by validating input against a strict schema before the function code runs.

### Migration and versioning

When a function's code changes, the project's version counter increments (because `load_project_code()` is called during compilation). The next request to the API triggers a recompile, which re-imports the module and re-registers the function. This means:

- Changing a function's implementation is immediate — no deployment needed
- Changing a function's `@function` decorator parameters requires reimporting the module
- Removing a function requires removing the file or the decorator — the registry does not auto-clean
- Adding a new function file to `code/<project>/functions/` automatically imports it and registers it

### Common pitfalls

1. **Forgetting to make a function async**: The `@function` decorator requires an async callable. A synchronous function raises `TypeError` at registration time.
2. **Using `input_fields` incorrectly**: Field definitions must match the schema format used by resources. Unknown field types raise `SchemaError`.
3. **Exceeding the timeout**: A function that runs longer than its `timeout` is cancelled. The `timeout` should be set generously enough for the function's worst-case execution time.
4. **Ignoring the depth limit**: A function that calls other functions should track its depth to avoid hitting the 8-level limit.
5. **Using `ctx.runtime` for side effects without error handling**: Network calls through `ctx.runtime` can fail. Use `try/except` and the retry mechanisms where appropriate.
6. **Not using `ctx.log()` for observability**: Without log entries, function executions are invisible in Studio's Observability. Always log important events.

**Data operations**: `resource_list`, `resource_get`, `resource_create`, `resource_update`, `resource_delete` all delegate to the environment's `Store`, which is obtained via `state.store(resource)`. The `Store` handles the policy gate, the database query, and the response transformation. `db_query` and `db_transaction` delegate to the environment's SQL source, with `is_read_only` enforcement on `db_query`.

**Cache**: `cache_get`, `cache_set`, `cache_delete`, and `cache_invalidate` all delegate to `sillo.cache`, which is namespaced with a `user:` prefix to prevent collisions between projects. The cache backend is Redis when configured, or an in-memory fallback.

**Events**: `emit` publishes a `PlatformEvent` to the platform event bus. The event is recorded synchronously in the database so nothing is silently dropped. The fan-out to subscribers happens asynchronously through the worker's queues.

**Jobs**: `dispatch_flow` and `dispatch_function` create `RunFlowJob` or `RunFunctionJob` instances and dispatch them through Sillo's work queue. The `delay` parameter allows scheduling the job to run after a specified number of seconds.

**Storage**: `storage_put`, `storage_read`, `storage_signed_url`, and `storage_delete` all delegate to `sillo.storage`, which is configured with a service credential. This means functions can read and write any bucket in the project.

**Mail**: `send_mail` delegates to `sillo.mail`, which dispatches a `SendMailJob`. The mail is sent using the environment's SMTP configuration.

**HTTP**: `http_request` uses Sillo's HTTP client with configurable timeout and retries. The client is connection-pooled, so multiple calls to the same host share connections.

**Platform**: `secret` reads from the environment's secret store, which is encrypted at rest. `call_function` delegates to the function registry. `identity_user` calls Akountz for user profile information. `check_policy` evaluates a policy against a context. `log` and `metric` write to structured logging and metrics stores.

### The scheduler's role

The scheduler process (`python -m app.scheduler`) plays an important role in function execution when functions are triggered by schedules. The `PlatformScheduler` class in `services/api/app/scheduler.py` reconciles the database's schedule records with the scheduler's registered jobs every 15 seconds. It builds a desired state from `Schedule` records and flow definitions that contain `trigger.schedule` nodes, then adds, removes, or re-registers jobs as needed.

When a schedule fires, the scheduler dispatches either a `RunFlowJob` or `RunFunctionJob` (depending on the schedule's target type), or emits an event (for event-targeted schedules). The firing is queued, not executed inline, so the scheduler can handle many schedules without blocking.

### The worker's role in function execution

The worker process consumes jobs from the queue and executes them. For function jobs (`RunFunctionJob`), the worker:

1. Deserializes the job payload
2. Resolves the function name to a handler using `get_function()`
3. Creates a `FunctionContext` with the job's input and the system auth context
4. Binds the platform context to the current task
5. Calls the function handler
6. Records the result (success or failure) in the job repository
7. Emits any events the function produced

The worker's concurrency is controlled by `WorkerOptions.concurrency` (default 4). Each worker process runs a pool of coroutines that consume from the queue simultaneously. The `sleep` parameter controls how long the worker waits between checking the queue when it is empty.

### Observability

Functions produce observable data through several channels:

- **Studio Observability**: Every request that triggers a function records the route, policy decision, and any events emitted. Function logs appear in the run trace.
- **Worker heartbeat**: The `WorkerHeartbeat` model tracks the worker's processed job count, concurrency, and status.
- **Failed job repository**: `RecordFailedJobRepository` stores failed function jobs with their exception details, so they can be inspected and retried.
- **Metrics**: The `Runtime.metric()` method increments named metrics with optional tags, which are aggregated by the metrics store.

### Security

Functions inherit the security model of the platform:

- **Policy gating**: HTTP invocations of functions are gated by the function's `policy` attribute, just like resource operations.
- **Context verification**: Every function invocation receives a verified `PlatformContext` through the `X-Pawabase-Context` header chain.
- **Secret isolation**: `ctx.runtime.secret()` only returns secrets for the function's project and environment. A function in project `acme` cannot access secrets from project `globex`.
- **Depth limit**: The 8-level depth limit on `call_function` prevents privilege escalation through recursive function calls.
- **Input validation**: `input_fields` prevent injection attacks by validating input against a strict schema before the function code runs.

### Migration and versioning

When a function's code changes, the project's version counter increments (because `load_project_code()` is called during compilation). The next request to the API triggers a recompile, which re-imports the module and re-registers the function. This means:

- Changing a function's implementation is immediate — no deployment needed
- Changing a function's `@function` decorator parameters requires reimporting the module
- Removing a function requires removing the file or the decorator — the registry does not auto-clean
- Adding a new function file to `code/<project>/functions/` automatically imports it and registers it

### Common pitfalls

1. **Forgetting to make a function async**: The `@function` decorator requires an async callable. A synchronous function raises `TypeError` at registration time.
2. **Using `input_fields` incorrectly**: Field definitions must match the schema format used by resources. Unknown field types raise `SchemaError`.
3. **Exceeding the timeout**: A function that runs longer than its `timeout` is cancelled. The `timeout` should be set generously enough for the function's worst-case execution time.
4. **Ignoring the depth limit**: A function that calls other functions should track its depth to avoid hitting the 8-level limit.
5. **Using `ctx.runtime` for side effects without error handling**: Network calls through `ctx.runtime` can fail. Use `try/except` and the retry mechanisms where appropriate.
6. **Not using `ctx.log()` for observability**: Without log entries, function executions are invisible in Studio's Observability. Always log important events.

### Further reading

- [Runtime protocol](/code/runtime) — the full `Runtime` interface
- [Extension routes](/code/extension-routes) — HTTP routes that bypass the flow engine
- [Flows or code](/code/flows-or-code) — the decision framework for choosing between flows and functions
- [Python extensions](/code/python-extensions) — adding custom blocks, functions, and routes via entry points
- [The mental model](/concepts/mental-model) — the five ideas behind Pawabase


## Additional detail section 21

The function system in Pawabase is deeply integrated with the platform's architecture. Each function goes through a rigorous lifecycle from registration to invocation to completion. Understanding this lifecycle is essential for building reliable, performant functions that integrate well with the rest of the platform.

### Registration details

When a module containing `@function` decorators is imported during `load_project_code()`, the `_loading_project` context variable is set to the project name. Each `@function` decorator reads this variable and uses it as the `project` field in the `FunctionSpec`. This ensures that functions are properly namespaced — a function named `"calculate_price"` registered by project `"acme"` is distinct from the same-named function registered by project `"globex"`.

The `_import_file()` function uses `importlib.util.spec_from_file_location()` to create a module spec from a file path, then `importlib.util.module_from_spec()` to create the module, and finally `spec.loader.exec_module()` to execute the module code. This is the same mechanism Python uses for loading source files directly, without requiring them to be in the Python path.

### The import pipeline

The import pipeline for project code follows a specific order. First, all `.py` files in `code/<project>/functions/` are imported in alphabetical order. Then, `policies.py`, `transformers.py`, and `routes.py` are imported if they exist. This ordering matters because `routes.py` depends on functions being registered (extension routes may call functions by name), and policies may reference functions.

Import errors are collected and reported, not raised. This is a deliberate design choice: one broken module in one project must not stop the API from serving every other project. If `code/acme/functions/billing.py` has a syntax error, the API continues to serve `acme`'s other functions and all other projects. The error is logged and stored in `ProjectCode.errors`, which Studio can display in its error reporting.

### Context propagation

When a function is invoked, the platform context is propagated through the `FunctionContext`. The `auth` field contains the caller's policy context, which is assembled by `build_policy_context()` in `pawabase_kit/policies/gate.py`. This context includes `auth`, `credential`, `request`, `project`, `env`, `record`, and `input` fields — everything a policy condition needs.

The `bind_context()` function stores the `PlatformContext` in a `contextvars.ContextVar`, which means it is available to any code running in the same async task. This includes nested function calls, async generators, and callback handlers. The context is automatically reset when the function invocation completes, preventing context leakage between concurrent requests.

### Error handling in depth

When a function raises an exception, the error handling depends on whether the call is inline or background. For inline calls, the exception is caught by the flow engine's `_execute_node()` method in `flows/engine.py`. If the block has an `error` edge and the exception is a `FlowError`, the flow follows the `error` handle. Otherwise, the exception is re-raised as a `FlowError` with the original exception type and message.

For background jobs, the exception is caught by the worker's job processing loop. The job is marked as failed, the exception details are stored in `RecordFailedJobRepository`, and the worker continues processing the next job. The failed job can be inspected in Studio and retried.

The `Runtime.call_function` method adds an additional layer of protection: the depth counter. Each nested `call_function` increments the depth, and if it exceeds 8, a `FlowError` is raised. This prevents a chain of mutually recursive function calls from consuming all available stack space and crashing the worker process.

### Input validation in depth

When `input_fields` are declared in the `@function` decorator, the platform validates the input using `validate_payload()` from `pawabase_kit/schemas.py`. This function compiles the field definitions into a Pydantic model using `compile_model()`, then validates the payload against that model.

The `compile_model()` function handles all the field types that resources use: `string`, `text`, `integer`, `number`, `boolean`, `datetime`, `date`, `uuid`, `email`, `url`, `json`, `array`, `object`, and `ref`. It also applies constraints like `min_length`, `max_length`, `minimum`, `maximum`, `pattern`, and `enum`. The resulting Pydantic model is cached, so validation after the first call is fast.

If validation fails, a `pydantic.ValidationError` is raised with field-level error details. This is caught by the function invocation logic and converted to a `FlowError` with status `422`. The error response includes the specific fields that failed validation and the reasons, which is the same format that Sillo uses for resource request validation.

### Runtime capabilities in depth

The `Runtime` protocol defines every capability a function can use. Each method in the `ApiRuntime` implementation delegates to a specific Sillo capability or platform service:

**Data operations**: `resource_list`, `resource_get`, `resource_create`, `resource_update`, `resource_delete` all delegate to the environment's `Store`, which is obtained via `state.store(resource)`. The `Store` handles the policy gate, the database query, and the response transformation. `db_query` and `db_transaction` delegate to the environment's SQL source, with `is_read_only` enforcement on `db_query`.

**Cache**: `cache_get`, `cache_set`, `cache_delete`, and `cache_invalidate` all delegate to `sillo.cache`, which is namespaced with a `user:` prefix to prevent collisions between projects. The cache backend is Redis when configured, or an in-memory fallback.

**Events**: `emit` publishes a `PlatformEvent` to the platform event bus. The event is recorded synchronously in the database so nothing is silently dropped. The fan-out to subscribers happens asynchronously through the worker's queues.

**Mail**: `send_mail` delegates to `sillo.mail`, which dispatches a `SendMailJob`. The mail is sent using the environment's SMTP configuration.

**HTTP**: `http_request` uses Sillo's HTTP client with configurable timeout and retries. The client is connection-pooled, so multiple calls to the same host share connections.

**Platform**: `secret` reads from the environment's secret store, which is encrypted at rest. `call_function` delegates to the function registry. `identity_user` calls Akountz for user profile information. `check_policy` evaluates a policy against a context. `log` and `metric` write to structured logging and metrics stores.
